#6993. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

#6989. Report: Cyber Attacks Increasingly Exploit Trusted Workplace Tools

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks. Approximately 30% of organizations monitored by the cybersecurity company experienced at least one attempt to impersonate IT support through Microsoft Teams during the first half of 2026. About one in every 990 external Teams contacts showed signs of someone pretending to be an internal IT worker.

Ontinue's report says the trouble is that these attacks blend in with ordinary workday communication, which makes them harder to catch.

"The majority of effective attacks today don't rely on sophisticated exploits. They rely on trust," Balazs Greksza, director of Advanced Threat Operations at Ontinue, said in the company's announcement.

Ontinue broke down how a typical attack works: An employee gets an unexpected Teams message or call from someone claiming to work in the organization's IT department. The person warns of a security issue, a software problem or an update that can't wait. Then they walk the employee through opening Windows Quick Assist, installing remote-access software, or running a PowerShell command. Following those steps can hand the attacker control of the employee's computer, often without the employee realizing it.

The approach is very similar to the Black Basta ransomware group, which helped popularize similar attacks in 2024. Unfortunately, Ontinue found the technique has since been adopted by other cybercriminal groups, including some with no known link to Black Basta.

Some threat agents are also combining tactics. They might first overwhelm an employee's inbox with unwanted e-mails, then reach out through Teams pretending to be IT support offering to fix the problem.

"Phishing no longer resides solely in your inbox. It has spread across the entire corporate communications environment," said Mika Aalto, co-founder and CEO of Hoxhunt.

Teams impersonation is only part of the problem. Ontinue also found attackers taking advantage of legitimate Microsoft sign-in processes. Between May and June, researchers identified 10 confirmed device-code phishing compromises across five customer tenants.

In these attacks, criminals trick employees into completing what appears to be a normal Microsoft authentication process. But doing so can give the attacker access to the employee's account and system without actually breaking their password.

The report also found attackers using blockchain-based infrastructure to hide malicious activity, accounting for more than 25% of confirmed malicious ClickFix findings.

"Attackers are no longer looking for gaps in your perimeter. They are looking for the systems your organization already trusts and finding ways to move through them undetected," said Shane Barney, chief information security officer at Keeper Security.

The security firm says that IT needs to keep a closer eye on unexpected Teams contacts, remote-support requests, suspicious PowerShell activity, and changes to authentication settings. Employees should also confirm sensitive requests through established internal channels, rather than relying on the name or account that appears on their screen.

Aviv Nahum, co-founder and CEO of Above Security, noted that AI is making these attacks more convincing by helping criminals imitate familiar voices, writing styles, and conversations.

"The security model therefore must shift from 'does this look real?' to 'can I independently verify that this person is who they claim to be?'" Nahum said.

The full Ontinue report can be found here.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6987. Sophos Launches Tool for Shadow AI Defense

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos. In a recent product announcement, the company highlighted the growing use of shadow AI, referring to AI tools and agents operating without formal organizational approval or oversight. The security firm cited research indicating that 69% of organizations suspect or have evidence of unauthorized AI use.

Separate research from Gartner and Cloud Security Alliance found that 82% of organizations discovered shadow AI agents in their environments during the previous year.

According to the security firm, organizations have struggled to identify which AI tools employees use, what information those tools access, and whether their use complies with security policies.

"Organizations are adopting AI faster than they can secure it, and most security teams can't answer three basic questions: which AI tools are running, what data is going into them, and whether that activity lines up with policy," said Kyle Falkenhagen, senior vice president of product management at Sophos.

The company also cited an IDC projection that the number of AI agents could reach 1.3 billion by 2028.

Sophos researchers reported observing attackers use AI agents within a customer's network to build and test nearly 80 malware modules.

The cybersecurity firm announced the general availability of Sophos AI Defense, a security offering designed to identify AI tools, monitor their activity, and enforce organizational security policies.

The product operates through Sophos Fusion, the company's cybersecurity platform, and uses information from devices, networks, identity systems, and browsers.

According to the company, AI Defense can inspect prompts, uploads, and other AI interactions to identify potentially risky activity and help prevent sensitive data exposure.

For more information, go to the Sophos site.

#6983. Microsoft Expands AI Security Platform to Address Agentic AI Security Risks

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

In a company blog post, Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, outlined how Microsoft plans to use Project Perception to monitor, manage, and contain what it calls "agentic insiders." These are AI agents deployed within an environment that can access data, use software tools, and make decisions without constant human supervision. While they can improve productivity, Microsoft warns that they also introduce security risks, even when they haven't been compromised by an attacker.

"Agents are unpredictable," Gallot wrote. "They will adapt as the context changes, may not behave as intended, and will collaborate and collude with other agents to reach their objectives, without needing to be compromised."

Project Perception was first introduced in July. The platform, which entered public preview Aug. 3, is designed to help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyber threats using AI-powered automation.

Instead of leaning only on traditional security alerts and manual investigations, Project Perception uses three kinds of specialized AI agents working together. Red team agents hunt for possible vulnerabilities, blue team agents look into suspicious activity, and green team agents step in to fix problems and strengthen defenses.

To spot threats and respond faster, the system pulls in security data from identities, endpoints, cloud environments, applications, and other sources.

Now Microsoft wants to use those same capabilities on the AI agents already running inside enterprise networks. Project Perception would find those agents, give them identities and permissions, and keep an eye on what they do across different systems. It would also keep assessing the risks that come with their actions.

Microsoft also described a plan for "Guardian Agents," specialized AI agents that supervise other agents and step in when their behavior gets risky. That might mean limiting an agent's permissions or blocking certain actions before they cause harm.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," Gallot wrote.

The initiative also reflects concerns raised by Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella in an Oct. 10 essay on AI security.

Nadella argued that enterprises should treat powerful AI models much like potential insider security risks, particularly when those models can access confidential information or perform critical business operations.

"We simply can't outsource responsibility for what intelligence does on our behalf," Nadella wrote.

He called for security controls that operate independently of AI models, including restricted permissions, detailed activity logging, and mechanisms that allow administrators to stop an agent's actions.

"In other words, we need to separate the supply of intelligence from the authority over it," Nadella wrote.

Microsoft admits that watching what AI agents do is only part of the answer. Gallot said that future security tools will also need to show why AI models make the choices they do, not just what actions they end up taking.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

#6979. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

Many enterprise breaches still begin with familiar weaknesses, according to a new report from SonicWall.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

#6974. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

Video game designers spend an enormous amount of time studying what keeps people engaged when something becomes difficult. They have become very good at helping people persist. Schools should pay attention.

A new report from Caylent, an AI-focused Amazon Web Services Premier Tier Services Partner, found that enterprises are already moving agentic AI beyond pilots and into production environments. At the same time, organizations are putting strict conditions around autonomy, making governance and control the next major challenge for enterprise AI adoption.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

#6970. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

#6967. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

Video game designers spend an enormous amount of time studying what keeps people engaged when something becomes difficult. They have become very good at helping people persist. Schools should pay attention.

A new report from Caylent, an AI-focused Amazon Web Services Premier Tier Services Partner, found that enterprises are already moving agentic AI beyond pilots and into production environments. At the same time, organizations are putting strict conditions around autonomy, making governance and control the next major challenge for enterprise AI adoption.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

#6964. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

#6963. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6961. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

#6959. Skyler Gisondo on His New Fashion Campaign—and Dressing Like Harrison Ford in the ’90s

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Primetime star is gearing up in Bonobos and getting ready for a very big 2027.

Skyler Gisondo is remarkably easy to talk to. If you’ve seen him on one of your various screens—whether as Decoy Dan from Primetime, Jimmy Olsen from Superman, or maybe just that meme of him from a decade-old red-carpet photo—you probably assumed as much. It’s the big smile, the friendly eyes, the freckles, the general sense of wholesomeness. But I can confirm it, because even though I know we’ve got a relatively brief window of time to discuss his new campaign with men’s fashion brand Bonobos over Zoom, we spend the first few minutes of our chat just … shooting the shit. It feels like catching up with a buddy you haven’t seen in a while. Relaxed. Natural.

Thankfully, the vibe continues as we start digging into matters of style. Gisondo tells me he’s always been interested in dressing well but hasn’t always known where to start. “I would see a cool dude walking down the street or something, and think, ‘Man, I want to look like that guy.’ But I never knew how. I always felt lost,” he says. He describes his 20s as a decade of “desperately trying on shoes that didn’t fit or something.”

He turned 30 recently, though, and “I really feel like over the last couple of years, I’ve homed in on my sense of style, what I feel good and confident in.” A very good time, then, to star in a fashion campaign—especially considering this seems like a breakout moment for the actor. He’s been working consistently since he was a kid, but his performance in Primetime as a young actor who gradually realizes just how toxic the entertainment industry can be has clearly launched him to another level. And he still has his upcoming role in Focker-in-Law this November and a couple more appearances as Jimmy Olsen (in Man of Tomorrow on the big screen and The People v. Gorilla Grodd on television) on the docket for 2027.

Over the course of our conversation, we touch on everything from dressing like a cowboy (a mistake) to using Harrison Ford in the ’90s as your style North Star (a very good idea). We also chat about the stuff he’s stolen from sets for his own wardrobe, the psychological validation of joining the Shrek franchise, and why, after Primetime, we can expect more creative big swings in the future. Read on for a few highlights.

This interview has been condensed and edited.

“It’s ultimately about your own confidence. I feel like the sweet spot for fashion is when what you look like reflects how you feel inside—when you get to this equilibrium and it allows you to be a more fully realized version of yourself. We all grow and our sense of fashion evolves with us but ultimately like I feel like there should be some current that runs through all of it. The stuff I’m drawn to is the more timeless, classic stuff. I look at Harrison Ford in the ’90s and I’m just like, it doesn’t get cooler, you know?”

“For a while, for whatever reason, me and every actor I know were like trying to look like a cowboy. And it just hit me one day: ‘None of us have ever worked a day in our lives on a cattle ranch, man.’ I don’t even know how to use a hammer! Like, what are we doing? Can we just be ourselves?”

“I’m pretty active. I surf and hike a lot when I’m at home. And I love walking; if I have an hour or two between meetings, I’ll just walk a few miles and take calls. I want something comfortable and functional, and I find a lot of vintage workwear just hits that and fits me well. And the story is so compelling to me. The idea of ‘Who’s worn this? What were they getting up to?’ ”

“What drew me to the campaign and to the clothes is that there’s a kind of timelessness to that style. My mom thought I looked amazing in the photos. My girlfriend too. My grandma and my sisters thought I looked great. It’s this very accessible and sort of classically cool thing they have going on. And I felt like me. I felt confident—so much so that I stole several items from the set.”

“With shows and movies, the fear is that it’s a one-of-one item that you might need back for another season. But that’s when you hit them with the ‘pretty, pretty please?’ You say, ‘I swear to God, I’ll guard it with my life.’ And they say, ‘Okay, but you can’t tell anybody.’ With a campaign, it’s probably easier because they’re excited for you to have it and represent. But probably 80 percent of my closet is basically just clothes stolen from characters and sets. It’s fun because you get to try on a bunch of versions of yourself and learn what you like.

“For instance, with Jimmy Olsen in the first Superman movie, they did a fantastic job with this retro-future thing. And then in The People v. Gorilla Grodd, they went a little more vintage-y in a way that felt more like me, Skyler, personally. I tried to steal almost the entire closet.”

“Well, right now I’m doing press for Primetime and Focker-in-Law. I’m doing another movie for A24 called Goblin that I’m not sure how much I can say about; I really don’t want to get fired. But I’m so excited. It’s the most insane script I think I have ever read, and it’s definitely a big swing. We have Man of Tomorrow coming out in the summer, and then Shrek 5. The number of texts I got when I got Shrek … ‘You made it, man! Oh my God! I had no idea you made it!’ I was like, ‘Thank you. Yeah, baby! Shrek 5!’

“But ultimately, it’s a very exciting moment because it’s the first time I have people asking, ‘What do you want to do?’ And I’m like, ‘Damn! I never really thought about that!’ I feel like Primetime lit a fire under me in a real way. And even though I’ve kind of been doing this my whole life, I feel a sense of drive that I’ve never felt really in this way before. So, yeah, man, I’m excited.”

#6958. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

#6954. Our Last Chance to Save This Country Begins Today. Let’s Not Waste It.

Don’t even start thinking about the 2028 presidential election. Start thinking about what we’re going to do tomorrow and the day after that.

(Optional Video Accompaniment to This Post)

It is a decade now since the president came down the golden escalator and the country went straight to hell. All we’ve done as citizens of a self-governing democratic republic is squabble over the seating in the handbasket. Until Tuesday. Tuesday was nut-cutting time. Tuesday felt—probably rightly—like the beginning of an extended last chance that the country will have to spend every waking hour pursuing.

Just on Monday, the president closed out his demented road show with a weird address in front of one of his administration’s most conspicuous blunders: the Reflecting Pool. Meanwhile, the Supreme Court, with his carefully manufactured conservative majority, gave the boot to his latest attempt to ratfck the California elections, and the court did so without even a hint of an explanation. Then the president capped a rich, full day by bombing an Iranian island in the Persian Gulf, although I guess he went a little bananas on his little boutique website.

Mama said there’d be days like this. But she never said there’d be so many. And all the rest of them are on us. If I can offer one piece of advice it would be this: Ignore anything written that references the 2028 presidential election. If things break the way the polls say they will, there will be so much concentration required to do what we can to ameliorate the damage this administration has done and is capable of doing over the next two years. Starting the 2028 horse-race coverage this week would be yet another crime against poor, battered journalism. As the late Senator Edward Kennedy told a Democratic convention long ago:

Run through the tape, America. Finish the race.

#6948. The 2026 Esquire Cologne Awards

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Whether you’re looking to make a big impression on a special occasion or just searching for a great everyday scent, these winners have you covered.

There’s never been a better time to be a guy who wants to smell good. The men’s fragrance market is booming, the scents are bolder than ever, and you can shop for pretty much anything you could possibly imagine—from long-lasting extracts for a big night out to easy-to-wear options that’ll fit right in at the office—with ease.

The only problem with all that abundance is the project of narrowing things down to a manageable number of scents that might work for you. Which is where we come in. This year, the team here at Esquire decided to go big for our annual Cologne Awards. Our fragrance experts called in hundreds of new releases over the course of the year, narrowing them down through extensive testing and internal debate before each creating their own short list of winners.

That’s when things got really interesting. Round by round, the Esquire Cologne Awards committee met to discuss potential winners, spritzing and testing again and again before finally producing the list you see here. These are the 40 best scents of the year. You might not connect with every one of them—but if you can’t find something you love here, well, good luck out there.

This has become one of our daily wears—and for good reason. It’s going to please pretty much anyone. Spicy and aromatic, this cologne is vanilla and amber forward with discernible notes of Iris germanica and lavender giving it a more floral edge. The result is sensual and soft but never unmasculine, thanks to the aromatic freshness. Plus, it comes packaged in a giant flask. There’s nothing manlier than having a giant flask (literally) of cologne behind your bathroom mirror.

Leather lovers, this one is for you. Tom Ford’s newest Ombré Leather Reserve blends notes of earthy, black leather with silver juniper and sandalwood, creating a balanced, rooted, yet refreshing take on Ford’s classic Ombré Leather eau de parfum. Inspired by the great American West and the mountains and deserts that constitute it, this scent is a perfect balance between refinement and rugged masculinity.

If you were around in the ’90s and spent some time at the mall, you probably remember Gap Dream. It was the most iconic scent of the original lineup, which was discontinued in the early aughts. But Gap has given the people what they want: a relaunch of its beloved fragrance line. Dream remains the hero of the collection, fresh and clean like a brand-new white T-shirt, with bright citrus, soft florals, and botanical musk.

Noble but knows how to fuck. A raw oud with fresh sensuality. The peach is jam-like, adding a softness to the suede accord. Velvet Mood is a leather fragrance with added warmth, texture, and juicy sweetness. (It recalls the intimate, sexual encounter in Call Me by Your Name. The one with the peach. You know it.) It’s captivating and aura-maxxing, not a blind buy for most. But we’re still on the side of biting the peach.

This cologne has one job, and that job is beach. It’s salty, woody, and as fresh as anything else we smelled this year. From the blond driftwood to the beach rose, coconut water, and sea salt, all the notes are reminiscent of tropical paradise. It may not be summer anymore, but the perfect summer scent has landed. Now that it’s in your possession, you can turn any day into a beach day.

Though it is totally within the Ranger Station wheelhouse of western-inspired cowpoke smells, it’s a new earthy floral. The easy choice would have been to mix sandalwood and ambergris with whiskey and call it another saloon smell. Instead the Ranger Station team gave it an iris root heart—soft and floral without losing its edge. It’s not betting the farm and drinking away the loss. It’s betting the farm, losing it, and stepping out those double swing doors to see a sunrise and smell a new dewy and green morning. You lost it all, but that was yesterday. Keep moving forward and the next win can’t be too far down the road.

It took four years to get right and was, according to the company, the last thing on which Giorgio Armani put his personal stamp of approval. And it’s not what you might expect if you’re thinking of the aquatic, mineral freshness of something like Acqua di Giò. I Will is a different animal—all wood and vanilla with bergamot to keep things from getting too dark and dense. It’s rich and modern and exactly what people will be talking about 20 years from now when they’re discussing the Italian fashion house’s other iconic scents.

Soft and sensual, Musc Pallida lingers like a whisper on your skin. Powdery iris and warm musk are the main components here, and they work together seamlessly. It’s light and airy but doesn’t fade quickly, and it smells good on pretty much anyone. Don’t go into Musc Pallida expecting your typical manly musk fragrance—it’s much more elegant and intimate.

Earthy sweetness, earthly delights. This fragrance from Byredo is sweet without ever venturing into gourmand territory. Even with its Madagascar vanilla and vanilla liquor accord at the base, it is punctuated with fougères and florals that give it complexity without steering it into being a spice bomb or a cognac cocktail. Instead it has a bright greenness and soft musk that can see the light of day and what’s to come. Simply fresh, refined with some sweetness—for a beautiful day.

A sophisticated blend of cinnamon, sage, and bergamot gives this fragrance a warm, spicy character. Cinnamon fragrances tend to be overpowering and reserved for nighttime, but this scent feels sophisticated and is a great daytime spice since the spicy top notes are balanced by a vanilla and praline base. It’s an elegant take on a spice-forward scent and a Nordstrom exclusive, which makes sense since Parfums de Marly is one of the retailer’s top-selling brands.

Creed’s Iris Debonair—a totally new offering, not a new expression—walks a wonderful line. It’s very gentlemanly with its cool and classic fougère heart. The opening and its transition into the middle is a lot of bergamot, absinthe, and lavender, making it seem like an expected (albeit exceptional) barbershop scent. But as it settles, iris starts to come to the forefront. It’s not the old-fashioned iris of Green Irish Tweed but a softer one that pairs well with a soft ambrette dry down. It has a true masculine and a true feminine side, and neither controls your lasting memory of the scent. Totally captivating.

Citrus and amber are not natural bedfellows, but this scent—a more highly concentrated version of the former Le Gemme Tygar—makes a very strong case for their marriage. It’s bright and grapefruit-y in one instant and then rich and woody in another. The more you smell it, the more the moniker starts to make sense. It is, after all, named after tigereye, a gemstone revered not for its sameness but for the striking impression that its contrasting bands of color create.

Fig fragrances are having a moment, but this new Granado EDP blows all other fig scents out of the water. Candied green fig reveals rich, jammy notes that are sweet but not in the typical syrupy way; it’s a lush, even fresh kind of sweet. Once you get hints of sweet dates, florals, and woods, it all combines to create a beautifully creamy gourmand.

It opens fruity and floral. A burst of raspberry gives way to peony, geranium, jasmine, magnolia. That might sound a little tough for some guys—but remember it’s just the beginning. Those notes soon give way to a base of deep, powdery musks. It’s not your typical masculine fragrance; it’s something a little more delicate and, though the scent is rooted in centuries of Chinese tradition, modern. We’d encourage you to give it a try. You might just find a new daily driver.

Before you spritz this woody scent, you have to admire the artistry of the bottle. Mind Games is known for its chess-inspired bottles, and Knight’s Tour is a great example of that. The scent inside is a sophisticated blend of lavender and saffron with spicy accents up top and smooth woods at the base. It’s a great go-to option for every day.

One Esquire editor made Ripe her scent of the summer after it launched in the spring. The reason? Its perfect balance of warm florals, fresh fruity notes, soft woods, and musks is lush and enticing. Have you ever thought about how notes of lychee, watermelon sorbet, green leaves, rose water, and creamy sandalwood work together? Spoiler alert: It’s pretty awesome.

Ojai is like waking up in a luxury hotel in California, sunlight peeking through the shades as fruity and floral notes dance around the room. Juicy, ripe mango and honey-like saffron are at the heart of this fragrance, giving it a sweet yet elevated quality. But it also has an earthy, amber-y depth to it, thanks to patchouli and frankincense at the base.

Put rhubarb in a fragrance and we’re there. Demon Dancer takes the tartness of rhubarb and blackcurrant and adds bright citrus, fresh peppermint, and radiant orange blossom for an exciting opening before it settles into a woody base of amber, musk, and tonka bean. When the cool, fresh top and heart notes reveal the warm base, it really is something to take in.

Imagine that feeling when you’re walking around a smog-choked city and find an unexpected oasis. It’s fresh, green, but with just that tiniest hint of something darker and deeper. That’s this scent. It’s wood and pepper and greenery and a reminder that even in the midst of urban sprawl, we can always find a way back to nature.

The Dolce & Gabbana classic is now available as an alcohol-free perfume gel, making it a travel-friendly option for sensitive skin. The curved metal applicator makes it easy to apply the fragrance directly to pulse points, while the pocket-sized packaging is perfect for on-the-go touch-ups. The gel formula also works as a great base for layering if you already own the original fragrance and want to extend the wear time of the cult-favorite citrus scent.

#6946. Aneha Reveals She and Cam Both Planned to Say “No” at Their ‘Love Is Blind’ Wedding; Producers Encouraged Them to Go Anyway

Per Aneha, this was filmed almost *two* years ago.

Aneha and Cam were one of the few couples to actually make it to the altar during Love Is Blind season 11, and ultimately they said “I don’t.” Aneha was technically the one to turn Cam down with a “not yet,” but she just revealed they actually discussed it ahead of time and were both planning to say no. So, why’d they even go through with the wedding? Apparently, production encouraged them to—just in case they changed their minds at the last second.

“Cam and I had talked about it, and we said on both of our parts that most likely, it’s a no, but we should still go to the altar if there’s even the slightest chance that we say yes,” Aneha explained to Vulture. “Chris Coelen, the creator, talked to us about that: He said people, on the day, sometimes go from a no to a yes. And it’s worked out. That’s what we were hopeful for. We said regardless of what the answer was going to be, we’re going to stay together, whether married or engaged. But the day of it was tough because you put everything on, your family’s there, your friends are there, you’re celebrating this beautiful moment.”

Aneha added that “There was a feeling of disappointment mixed in with relief” when she turned Cam down, but that “I have to trust my gut. It didn’t feel right. I felt content, because we had already discussed that no matter what happens, we found each other through this amazing experience for a reason. We talked about being together in a more traditional relationship and seeing how that works. He could actually meet my family. They live in California, so it wasn’t the easiest thing for them to come meet Cam. They are shy and traditional in terms of how to marry.”

Love Is Blind season 11 was filmed almost two years ago, according to Aneha, and she and Cam are both currently single. “I felt like I almost let myself down,” she said. “I wish we had more time, maybe another month. I feel like I would have felt stronger in my opinion either way.”

The pair recently reunited during the reunion, so tune in on Sunday to find out where they stand.

#6944. Patrick Stewart Was a Serious Watch Guy. Here Are 5 of the Late Hollywood Legend's Best.

From a treasured Rolex to his beloved IWC, the late actor had a lifelong appreciation for the art of watchmaking.

“Someone once told me that time was a predator that stalked us all our lives," begins Captain Jean-Luc Picard, played by Sir Patrick Stewart in Star Trek Generations, in one of his most memorable lines. "I rather believe that time is a companion who goes with us on a journey, reminds us to cherish every moment, because it will never come again.”

Those musings on time feel particularly poignant now, following the British actor's death. There’s something rather comforting about the idea of time as a companion, particularly when you consider Stewart’s lifelong fascination with the very objects we use to measure it.

Because, as it turns out, Patrick Stewart was quite the watch guy. Not simply someone who happened to wear an expensive timepiece on the red carpet, but a genuine enthusiast with an appreciation for the craftsmanship, history, and ingenuity behind them. He bought his first watch at 18 and, by his own admission, never threw one away.

“The watch industry never ceases to amaze me,” he told the Fondation de la Haute Horlogerie in 2019. “Because a watch is more than just an object that gives the time. Behind every watch there is an idea, a design, a concept, an innovation, and the watchmaker's desire to create something that is greater than anything that has ever existed before.”

It's an enthusiasm that stayed with him throughout his life, from the Rolex he reportedly spent almost all his savings on after arriving in Hollywood to his enduring affection for IWC Schaffhausen. And, naturally, there were some rather lovely watches along the way.

So, in celebration of an extraordinary career and a life well lived, we've taken a look back at some of Patrick Stewart's finest watch moments.

Perhaps the most meaningful watch in Stewart's story is one for which we don't have an exact reference. Long before he became one of Hollywood's most recognizable actors, Stewart was given a rather unexpected piece of career advice by the actress Eva Marie Saint. She advised him that appearances mattered, and that an expensive watch might help convince producers he was already a successful actor. So, she took him to a Beverly Hills jeweller, where he picked out a Rolex. The only problem? It cost him his entire savings. “I was eating spaghetti and boiled potatoes for months after that!” he recalled in a 2019 interview with the Fondation de la Haute Horlogerie.

It's touching anecdote given Stewart's modest upbringing. Having grown up in poverty, he admitted that it took him years to feel comfortable spending money on beautiful things. But eventually, he came to realize there was nothing wrong with enjoying the rewards of his success.

Tissot Le Locle Powermatic 80 to the 2017 Canadian Grand Prix

One of Stewart's most entertaining watch moments came at the 2017 Canadian Grand Prix, when he joined Daniel Ricciardo on the podium and celebrated the Australian driver's third-place finish by drinking champagne from his racing boot. Yes, really.

And while most people were understandably distracted by the sight of Captain Picard downing champagne from a sweaty shoe, there was a rather lovely watch on his wrist, a Tissot Le Locle Powermatic 80. The reasonably priced Swiss automatic features a silver guilloché dial, Roman numerals, a black leather strap, and an impressive 80-hour power reserve. Proof that even a relatively affordable dress watch can look the part.

Long before his appearances at watchmaking galas, Stewart was already making a convincing case for the understated dress watch. In this archival portrait, the actor wears a small, gold-tone, rectangular timepiece, seemingly a Cartier Tank, on a dark leather strap. Paired with a pale linen-style suit and a knitted white top, it's a particularly handsome combination.

First introduced in 1917, the Cartier Tank has become one of watchmaking's most enduring designs, worn by everyone from Andy Warhol to Muhammad Ali. Its appeal lies in its simplicity: a rectangular case, elegant proportions, and a design that has barely needed changing in more than a century. And judging by this photograph, Stewart understood the appeal of a good dress watch long before his later affection for IWC became public knowledge.

IWC Portofino Automatic to the 2018 Academy Awards

When it came to dressing for the red carpet, Stewart understood the appeal of keeping things classic. At the 90th Academy Awards in 2018, he opted for an IWC Portofino Automatic, reference IW356504, a rather beautiful dress watch that proved you don't need an enormous, diamond-covered timepiece to make an impression. Housed in a 40mm case of 18-carat rose gold, the watch features a silver-plated dial, slim gold hour markers, and a discreet date window at three o'clock. It's finished with a dark brown alligator leather strap and powered by an automatic movement. There's nothing particularly flashy about it, which is precisely what makes it so appealing.

IWC Portugieser Chronograph Classic at the 2017 SIHH Gala

By 2017, Stewart's appreciation for fine watchmaking had brought him into the company of some of the industry's most celebrated names. That January, he attended IWC Schaffhausen's Decoding the Beauty of Time gala in Geneva alongside his wife, Sunny Ozell, sporting a rather handsome IWC Portugieser Chronograph Classic.

First introduced in 2013, the reference 3904 is a particularly elegant piece of watchmaking. Featuring a 42mm case, distinctive Arabic numerals, and a railway-track minute scale, it's a slightly sportier proposition than the Portofino. Inside sits IWC's automatic calibre 89361, complete with a flyback chronograph function and an impressive 68-hour power reserve. The evening celebrated the Swiss manufacturer's renewed Da Vinci collection, but Stewart's choice of wristwear showcased another of IWC's most recognizable designs. And given that he once named IWC as his favorite watchmaker, it's hardly surprising that he had such good taste.

#6943. Pierpaolo Piccioli Departs Balenciaga After 1 Year

The designer joined the house in 2025 after 25 years at Valentino.

Pierpaolo Piccioli is leaving Balenciaga after just one year at the helm. The news, announced today by parent company Kering, brings his brief tenure at the house to a close.

“Pierpaolo is an exceptional couturier and creative talent for whom we have the greatest respect,” Kering CEO Luca de Meo said in a statement. “Our ambition is to build on Balenciaga’s avant-garde spirit, honor its remarkable heritage, and continue to push creative boundaries, further establishing the house as a powerful and relevant voice.”

Piccioli joined Balenciaga in July 2025 after a transformative 25-year tenure at Valentino. The designer succeeded Demna, who oversaw a complete rejiggering of the brand’s house codes. Piccioli’s vision for the Spanish house focused less on the streetwear-inflected aesthetic that Demna championed, and more on a romantic, craft-minded approach. As he had at Valentino, the designer bridged the past and present to imagine something softer and more personal for the house.

His debut couture offering in July imbued archival references like the sack dress and the chou wrap with his signature approach to color. He also oversaw buzzy projects, including A New York Minute, a Celine Song-directed campaign starring Sarah Pidgeon, and collaborations with Euphoria creator Sam Levinson and Manolo Blahnik.

“We are deeply grateful for Pierpaolo’s contribution to Balenciaga,” Balenciaga CEO Gianfranco Gianangeli said. “Through his vision, he reaffirmed the couture origins of the house, bringing his considered approach to craftsmanship, construction, and creativity. I would like to sincerely thank him for his dedication, generosity, and collaboration.”

Piccioli’s final Balenciaga collection, which mixed couture techniques with everyday silhouettes, took its bow at Paris Fashion Week in early October. What’s next for the designer is still a question mark, and Kering has yet to name his successor.

#6940. 7 Peter Millar Staples That Work Wherever the Day Takes You

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Good clothes, fewer decisions. What’s not to like?

Getting dressed for just about anything is a whole lot easier when you have the right essentials in your closet. Whether you’re heading into the office, catching a flight, or packing for a weekend away, a few well-chosen pieces can take you from one plan to the next without having to give your outfit much thought.

That’s the appeal of Peter Millar’s Crown Collection, which brings together classic menswear with thoughtful details, quality fabrics, and a relaxed approach to dressing well. There’s an emphasis on comfort and practicality, but never at the expense of looking polished. From tailored trousers and half-zips to quilted layers and other everyday favorites, these are pieces you'll find yourself reaching for again and again.

Ahead, seven picks from the collection, available at Bloomingdale’s, that deserve a spot in your closet. Because looking good shouldn’t require a whole lot of effort.

A chore coat is a great casual Friday-to-weekend staple when you want to look put together without feeling too dressed up. This navy style has a relaxed, cardigan-like feel, with a classic collar and patch pockets that work just as well with a T-shirt and jeans as they do with a button-down and chinos.

The Essex Vest is an iconic piece within Peter Millar’s Crown Collection and is designed as a versatile layering option for fall that works across a range of occasions. This black diamond-quilted style offers an extra layer of warmth without the bulk of a jacket, making it just as useful for your morning commute as it is for a round of golf or an afternoon apple picking.

Few pieces strike the balance between comfort and looking sharp quite like a good half-zip. This navy pullover is comfortable enough for a long flight but polished enough to wear over a collared shirt with tailored trousers. Prediction: You’ll want it in multiple colors.

A quilted coat is timeless, especially in a rich, dark olive shade. This one has a classic countryside feel, but its clean shape looks just as at home on city streets with jeans and boots or layered over a button-down and trousers. A bottle opener tucked inside the left interior pocket adds a handy signature touch. It’s a smart choice for chilly days that don’t call for a heavy overcoat.

Utilitarian pants are a wardrobe icon, and this five-pocket design offers a slightly more refined take on the classic. The smooth sateen fabric and rich blue color give them a polished look that works with everything from a polo at the office to a crisp shirt and loafers for dinner.

Every man’s shoe collection should include a pair of driving loafers, particularly when comfort and looking elegant are equally important. These walnut-brown suede drivers feature classic penny-loafer detailing and flexible, rubber-studded soles, making them a natural choice with chinos, tailored trousers, or jeans.

A checked button-down is a classic for a reason, but how about one with a little more personality? This soft twill style features a small-scale pattern and subtle grapeseed color that look particularly great with navy, gray, or khaki. Designed to be tucked in, it pairs easily with tailored trousers for work or sateen five pocket pants for a more relaxed look.

#6938. Kendall Jenner Reveals the $35 K-Beauty Gua Sha Cream She Uses to De-Puff, Sculpt, and Define

It’s sold out in store, but here’s where to shop it online.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

In a skin care space so saturated, it’s refreshing to see a brand get something so right. The product in question? Anua’s Collagen + Retinol Refining Cream. The K-beauty cream has been around since March, but with such burgeoning popularity, it’s been near-impossible to track down.

So why the sudden popularity, and why now? This isn’t just a product that’s risen to fame through celebrity influence. Kendall Jenner (who is a brand ambassador) recently posted a TikTok demonstrating how she uses the sculpting tool as a “post-working detox.” It’s sold out on multiple occasions, proving that its popularity goes beyond a Jenner approval—it’s a brilliant, budget-friendly product that delivers.

This cream is more than just innovative packaging. Beyond the gua sha-style applicator, it cocktails the ultimate trifecta of ingredients designed to smooth, firm, and boost the skin’s elasticity: retinol, collagen, and peptides.

Let’s break that down, starting with retinol: the gold standard for tightening and minimizing fine lines. It uses encapsulated retinol, which, while that may sound like technical jargon, is what makes it so effective and gentle to use. Essentially, it wraps the vitamin A molecule inside a protective polymer to ensure a slow and safe delivery to the deeper layers of your skin. This way, it’s less likely to cause surface-level irritation.

Moving on to collagen. A collagen-pumped moisturizer or mask is not hard to come by. But, despite what the bottle may tell you, even the most expensive players won’t miraculously boost your collagen levels—nor will this cream. The molecule is just too large to be used in topical skin care, so, in this instance, the inclusion of collagen—as well as plumping peptides—is simply used for hydration purposes. They also contribute to the sensorial experience of the cream: it’s rich yet never greasy, lending the perfect amount of lightweight slip to glide the tool over your skin.

The K-beauty skin care scene has always managed to one-up us, whether that’s through innovative formulas or smart packaging—and in this case, both. Cleverly, the cream allows you to combine the sculpting benefits of a gua sha with your moisturizing routine.

The metal, W-shaped tool is designed to seamlessly glide over your skin while slowly dispensing the cream as it goes. In upward strokes, simultaneously squeeze and slide the tool over the contours of your face. Work upwards from the base of your neck, then move on to your jawline, cheekbones, and up to your temples. Repeat this motion three to five times before moving on to the next area.

Move the tool up and down your neck to soothe “tech neck” (stiffness in the neck from too much time looking down at your phone—a sadly relatable truth for us all). But for the rest of your face, the trick is to always glide in an upward motion to gently de-puff and help ease redness.

As with any retinoid, start slow by introducing the cream into your routine three times a week—and temporarily pause any other actives until your skin adjusts to the ingredient. It is cushioned by the peptides, but it’s always good to ease into actives. And, of course, always use an SPF in the mornings.

So while it may not deliver the same sculpting results as seen through a professional lymphatic drainage facial, this is a great alternative to temporarily decrease puffiness until your next appointment calls.

#6936. The Republican Party Failed Deliciously Last Night. Now the Real Work Begins.

Lawyer up, MAGA. Your hell has only just begun.

And then the folks on the Morning Joe show mournfully deplored Mr. Paine’s divisive rhetoric.

Man, just fck that noise. Line me up behind Representative Robert Garcia, the incoming chairman of the House Oversight Committee, who greeted the epic ass kicking of every evil loosed upon the world by the current president, the MAGA movement, and all of the cowards and enablers with a simple two-word message:

(And that is not even to mention that Representative Jamie Raskin will be chairing the House Judiciary Committee.)

Ignore all calls for conciliation. Turn a deaf ear to the impotent ghosts of Clintonian triangulation. Turn the last shovelful of dirt on moderation in pursuit of thieves and brigands. Accept the incredible momentum that has risen behind you from across the country and use it to do two things at once. Ameliorate the very real problems of affordability, and cleanse the money changers from the temple. There’s a lot of work to be done, but that is what the country demanded on Tuesday night. Tyranny, like hell, is not easily conquered.

And, at this writing, they’re still counting ballots for the United States Senate in Mississippi. Goddamn.

There are elements to the landslide that are worthy of their own posts, and we’ll get to them as the day goes on. But here are some other highlights of the Night of the Great Reckoning.

What seems like decades ago, the Republicans around the country came up with a brilliant idea: Let’s do an aggressive campaign of midterm gerrymandering so as to eliminate Black and/or liberal legislators now that the Supreme Court has fired the starting gun for the new Jim Crow. Say what you will about Hakeem Jeffries, but he rose to this challenge. He sent out word to states with Democratic-majority legislatures to get to work on their own plans. And he helped make an arcane legislative process into a national issue. And, on Tuesday, the GOP’s grand design failed utterly. From Axios:

And as a tasty bit of lagniappe, Representative Richard Hudson, the chairman of the National Republican Congressional Committee, lost his seat in North Carolina. (North Carolina was a notable GOP bloodbath.)

It was a big night for decency. Vivek Ramaswamy will now fade into luxurious obscurity. The ghastly racist campaign against Abdul El-Sayed in Michigan failed. Ohio congressman—and alleged domestic abuser—Max Miller will have all the time he needs now to answer depositions. And Representative Scott DesJarlais, who should have lost his seat years ago for being a complete creep, finally did so, to Victoria Broderick, whom he beat by more than 40 points two years ago. And Allison Montgomery became the first trans person elected to the state legislature in Alabama.

It was a very bad night for congressional crackpots. Representative Virginia Foxx is down in the North Carolina cataclysm. Representative Victoria Spartz of Indiana lost early on. Lauren Boebert is, at this writing, locked in a life-and-death struggle with Democratic candidate, and retired rear admiral, Eileen Laubacher. Two of the prime congressional suspects in the January 6 insurrection, Andy Biggs of Arizona and Scott Perry of Pennsylvania, have been retired from public life.

And they’re still counting in Mississippi, where they don’t allow recounts in Senate races.
