#6935. The Wicker Husband Is Attentive, Devoted, and Made of Branches. The Bar Is...Interesting

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

“Monster smut” is a booming category, “Creature Cocks” are their own category of sex toys, and right now Wicker has a 94 percent score on Rotten Tomatoes. And while I know people have always been into scales, wings, tentacles, and more, the actual material and texture aspect of wicker really has me stumped (pun definitely intended).

I made it a point to watch Wicker as soon as it came out. The trailer looked fun and exciting and unlike anything I’ve seen before. And at the end of the day, I love a good romance—even when it is between woman and basket. But I couldn’t help but wonder if there was something deeper going on with the buzz around the movie. A referendum on modern romance, perhaps? But where do the branches fit in?

“I think it says something quite revealing that being nice, considerate, and sexually attentive can be presented as the ultimate package,” Anna Richards, sex educator and founder of Frolicme.com—an ethical erotica site—tells Cosmopolitan. “These shouldn't be extraordinary qualities; they should be the foundations of a healthy relationship.”

She explains that women want to feel desired, respected, and understood, without having to sacrifice their own in the process of attaining these things. And the husband in Wicker, played by Alexander Skarsgaard, is all of these things. He is attentive, he fixes things around the house (without being asked, most importantly), and he is a vigorous lover.

“The fantasy might be an exaggerated fictional man, but the underlying desires are very real,” Richard says.

But in the current state of heterosexual dating, the growing sentiment for a while now is that women are not getting these basic, bare-minimum attributes out of the men they date.

And the attitudes aren't improving with younger generations: A 2026 global study by Ipsos and King's College London found that 31 percent of Gen-Z men believe a wife should always obey her husband, while 33 percent think husbands should have the final say on important decisions. Meanwhile, according to Pew Research Center, 66 percent of women under 50 who have used dating apps have experienced at least one form of unwanted behavior on the platforms, including unsolicited sexual messages, continued contact after rejecting someone, name-calling, or threats. So, between the manosphere's growing influence, the normalization of misogynistic attitudes, and the very real unpleasantness of dating straight men, it's perhaps not so surprising that women are finding romance in increasingly unconventional places.

Couples therapist Doriel Jacov, JD, LCSW explains that the socialization of men today plays a role. From childhood, he says, boys are taught to be strong and self-sufficient, without being given much insight into meeting someone’s emotional needs. Women, on the other hand, are expected to do it all: be present, attuned to others’ emotions, and handle everything on their own.

“Anger and overworking have historically been more socially accepted ways for men to express emotion than vulnerability,” Jacov says. “As a result, many women may find their emotional needs unmet, particularly as it relates to vulnerability and emotional presence.”

So when you see the Skarsgaard take the time to learn from the Fisherwoman (Olivia Coleman), act on her needs, and prioritize her happiness, the fact that he’s made of branches really feels like an irrelevant, secondary detail.

But, it really does need to be acknowledged that a made-to-order husband isn’t the healthiest thing, and not only because wicker isn’t super safe for your vagina. (I mean look what’s happening to the people who are in relationships with basically that, aka chat bots.) Deep down, we know that a great partnership includes two imperfect people working together to support one another through respect and love. But we can all learn from the Wicker Husband, regardless.

“The Wicker Husband is gentle, attentive and devoted, and his tenderness unsettles the whole village,” therapist Silvia Dutchevici, LCSW says. This is because even the men of the village think that someone who is a good partner is a rare sighting!

“That may be what so many of us are longing for: not a man under a woman’s control, but a man who is free to be kind, present, and vulnerable.”

#6933. An Expert Guide to Nail Health, According to Professionals

Here’s exactly how to keep them strong and healthy

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As manicure trends evolve with new lacquers, changing shapes, and viral techniques, beauty lovers often compromise nail health for aesthetics. While many nail formulas have improved over the years to prioritize strength, uniformity, and finish, experts say incremental habits—from lifestyle shifts to resisting the urge to peel off chipped polish—can ultimately play the biggest role in keeping your nails in excellent condition.

“The future of manicures isn’t just about the latest color trends or how long your gel polish lasts, but how well we care for the nails underneath,” Krisztina van der Boom, founder of DryBy, previously told Harper’s Bazaar UK. Hand, foot, and nail expert Margaret Dabbs agrees, urging nail enthusiasts to “Adopt a dedicated nail care routine and stick to it.” But what does an effective routine actually look like? Along with keeping your nails clean and hydrated with an emollient moisturizer, like Caudalíe’s Vinotherapist Hand and Nail Cream, professionals suggest keeping a cuticle oil, such as Dior’s Huile Abricot, in your bag for quick touch-ups.

“Cuticle oil is the one product I would never skip,” says Peach Bling founder Sandra Wei. “Look for a formula with jojoba oil, which has small enough molecules to absorb into the nail and surrounding skin.” Wei encourages her clients to apply it daily, ideally before bed, and to treat it as a small ritual. “A rich hand cream and gloves for dishwashing and cleaning also make a real difference,” continues Wei, “since water and harsh detergents are among the most common causes of dry, weak nails.” Maintaining nail and cuticle hydration is also key for mature nails, as they tend to grow more slowly and hold less moisture as we age, which can contribute to brittleness and vertical ridges.

Soft Services Theraplush Overnight Repair Treatment

Dior Huile Abricot Nutritive Serum for Nails and Cuticles

Professionals have debated whether it’s safe to trim your cuticles, since cuticles guard nail beds against bacteria and infection; that's why they often advise pushing them back instead of removing them entirely. “As long as you're not cutting any live skin and damaging your cuticles, gently pushing them back and buffing away excess skin will actually help improve the health and the appearance of your cuticles and will help make your manicure last as long as possible while looking salon-quality,” says Olivia Van Iderstine, vice president of education and manicure trend expert at Olive & June. Prep your fingertips with cuticle oil or a soak in warm, soapy water before a manicure, then use a disposable orangewood stick or stainless steel pusher, such as JinSoon’s HyperCare Cuticle Pusher + Reducer, to improve polish cohesion and create an elongated nail look.

Consistent, gentle filing can also keep nails strong, regardless of your shape preference, because a smooth surface guards against unwanted splits, rips, and tears that can cause long-term damage. While there’s nothing wrong with a budget-friendly emery board, professionals say investing in the right tools provides the right resistance without compromising your nails' integrity. Dabbs is a fan of crystal nail files because they can help prevent micro-splits that lead to peeling and breakage, though the best results depend on not overfiling.

And if you usually opt for gel polish manicures, they don’t have to be a deal breaker for nail health. Several options, from builder gel to Japanese gel and more, provide longevity, durability, and style without weakening natural nails.

“Japanese gel systems are known for their precision and for their focus on preserving the natural nail,” continues Wei. “The prep is minimal and gentle, so we avoid heavy buffing of the nail plate. The gel itself is applied in fine, structured layers that are flexible and sit close to the natural nail.” Peach Bling prioritizes formulas that are free of HEMA and TPO, two ingredients commonly linked to gel allergies and sensitivities. “The result is a manicure that looks refined, wears beautifully, and treats the nail underneath with care,” explains the founder. Bazaar editors love builder gel, as it works like Japanese gel, supporting natural nails with a rounded apex to reinforce their sturdiness.

Experts advise keeping a gel set on for no longer than four weeks, as regrowth changes the nail's balance, ultimately causing unwanted stress and making breaks more likely. Once gel polish begins to lift or crack, it’s often a sign that removal is necessary—and that process can often be more damaging than simply wearing a gel formula in the first place. “What protects nail integrity is how the gel is applied and, even more importantly, how it is removed,” adds Wei. “With careful, gentle removal, there is no need to take a ‘break’ between appointments.”

For more than 150 years, Harper’s Bazaar has been the preeminent fashion and beauty resource for women at every age. We cover what’s new and what’s next in beauty by working with the world’s leading authorities in dermatology, plastic surgery, cosmetics, skincare, hair care, and fragrance. Every story we publish has been thoroughly researched and vetted by our team of beauty editors and industry experts.

#6929. I Tried Hailey Bieber’s Favorite Creatine Gummies for Two Months, and I Have Thoughts

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Why these candylike gumdrops have converted me into a creatine lover

The supplement landscape has never been murkier, more confusing, or more overwhelming. While I have a pretty extensive wellness regimen, it feels like there are a million and one powders, pills, and gummies I should be adding to my routine. One supplement that keeps coming into the spotlight is creatine.

I first heard about creatine from my 62-year-old mother, who waxed poetic about the supplement. Then, the influencer of all influencers, Hailey Bieber, posted on Instagram a few weeks later a creatine gummy from Arrae. I thought: That’s it. I’m taking the creatine plunge.

“Creatine works like an energy recycler,” says Roxanne Pero, a Dallas-based obstetrician and gynecologist and part of O Positiv’s Medical Advisory Board. “Every cell in the body needs energy, and that energy comes from a molecule called ATP, or adenosine triphosphate. Creatine helps the body regenerate ATP more quickly, especially when energy runs low, and especially in tissues that use a lot of it, like muscle and the brain.”

As someone who works out five times a week, often in heated classes that push my body to depletion, I figured I was the perfect candidate for creatine. But it’s actually one of those supplements anyone can benefit from; it kicks in at moments when our cells need more energy—not just during strenuous exercise, but also during sleep deprivation and stress, which everyone experiences at some point. It’s even recommended for perimenopause, menopause, and postpartum.

“Active women in their 20s and 30s may benefit from improved training performance, while women approaching menopause or experiencing it may be interested in supporting muscle strength as part of a broader strategy that includes resistance training and adequate protein,” says Rachel Brief, a registered dietitian and wellness advisor at the Lanby.

Recently, the supplement has gained traction among women in their 40s and above. “As a woman enters menopause and postmenopause, the decrease in estrogen contributes to age-related muscle and strength loss,” says Shilpa Raut, the vice president of R&D at Cymbiotika, who holds a master’s degree and a PhD in pharmaceutical sciences. Creatine can help women maintain muscle mass, support steady metabolic health, and improve cognitive resilience, which often declines with age.

If you’re wondering why everyone isn’t already taking this magic supplement, well, I was too. Creatine has long been associated with gym bros, and it has a bad reputation for causing bloating, which is not a risk I’m willing to take as a girl with a sensitive gut. I also wasn’t excited about consuming a chalky white powder. But Arrae’s Tone gummies tout all of the benefits without the bloat, and they come in a candylike form. “Creatine had always been marketed to men, because no one had ever presented it in a way that felt like it was made for women,” Arrae’s cofounder Siffat Haider tells me. “Our customers adopted it immediately, and that demand is why we kept building on the flavors.”

The one caveat with creatine is that you need to drink water to get the full benefits. “Creatine draws water into muscle cells, and that is part of how it works,” says Michael Fredericson, a sports-medicine physiatrist, professor at Stanford University, and Elysium Scientific Advisory Board member. If you’re dehydrated, creatine will pull your body’s existing water supply into your muscles, causing dehydration and hindering normal bodily functions.

I’m now two months into my experiment with Arrae Tone. Whenever I tell people that I started taking creatine, they ask me the same question: “Does it actually work?” My verdict: I don’t plan to stop taking it any time soon. Experts say creatine is especially beneficial as a supplement to strength-training workouts, so I was excited to see how it amplified my workouts. Even a few weeks into taking creatine, I noticed more definition in my abdominals and arms, areas where I’ve historically struggled to see muscle.

The Arrae gummies have no chalky taste and are easy to swallow. I take a lot of different powders and pills, so having a delicious gummy is a nice change of pace. Plus, the dosage is only 3.75 to 5 grams (depending on whether you take three or four gummies), which is on the lower end of the spectrum and has been proven to be effective without causing the dreaded bloating. And it’s creatine monohydrate, which is the gold standard of creatine, Brief explains. Still, they’re not my only form of creatine. On days when I’d rather add a powder to a smoothie or my water to boost hydration, I love the Elysium Health powders; they’re only 5 grams, so they’re easy on my stomach, and they come in delicious fruity flavors that taste like nostalgic lemonade.

#6923. Zillennials Don’t Feel the Need to Grow Up, and Club Kid Knows Why

Jordan Firstman’s deferred coming-of-age tale speaks to the realities of living in an unwieldy time

I’m 29, an age that has become a catalyst for conversations about what it means to be an adult, settling down (or not), and—perhaps above all—how to show up for my own well-being. (Do I need to party less?) It’s a volley of topics that are often the subject of yearly think pieces, even when we know the conclusions they’ll reach. Millennials and Zillennials feel pressured by older generations to live a conventional and ambitious life, even as they’ve squeezed young people’s hope for a future.

The New York Times detailed this dilemma just last month, speaking to 30-somethings across New York City who felt discouraged by their situation and, to put it plainly, left behind. The article pointed out that the long-running Harvard Youth Poll found that for the first time ever, most young Americans don’t believe they will surpass their parents financially. In the last 30 years, the cost of living has skyrocketed, and, today, mounting loans and debt among young people have made a house feel out of reach. Looking even younger, Gen Z is feeling the brunt of the economy, with Fortune finding that two thirds of parents say their Gen Z adult kids rely on them (and Playboy going so far as to connect the housing crisis to the sex decline). Why stop partying if the rent’s too high, if there’s no reasonable expectation that you’ll find security, buy a house, or live easily past 2050?

This growing frustration is likely why audiences—including myself—are connecting so deeply with influencer-turned-director Jordan Firstman’s sensational feature debut, Club Kid. The film offers a tender look at arrested development and unexpected fatherhood, but, above all, it delivers an incisive look at the struggle to let go of youth when the world feels increasingly grim. The idea of partying instead of worrying is alluring: to exist in a space free from responsibility. Peter (Firstman), a 30-something party promoter deeply enmeshed in New York’s underground queer club scene, lives in the apartment his late mother left him and sells drugs on the side, and his only real daily concern is recovering for the next party. The moment he crosses the threshold of any club, he plunges into a fugue state fueled by drugs and sex. Peter clings to his youth (his name aptly echoing Peter Pan syndrome), shirks all responsibilities, and actively defers a much-needed inward look.

Peter’s wake-up call arrives in the form of a 10-year-old boy named Arlo (Reggie Absolom)—a son he unknowingly fathered years after hooking up with a man who insisted they turn their dalliance into a threesome with a woman. A decade after that fateful night, the woman’s best friend (played with drug-fueled frenzy by Kirby Howell-Baptiste) shows up at his door, a young child in tow, with news that the mother has died by suicide and Peter needs to step up. It’s a parable tailor-made for a worn-down generation that sees no reason to stop the party. It takes a ketamine stork of sorts to change course.

Club Kid revels in the growing pains. Firstman avoids reducing the conflict to a simple clash over parenting Arlo or his personal struggle to break free from the party lifestyle; instead, Club Kid unfolds as a tender parallel coming-of-age story, where Peter grows up as he learns to parent Arlo, a quiet, curious kid grappling with the loss of his mother. Firstman embraces the missteps, letting us see the beauty of his life as it intermingles with his newfound parenting. The whirlwind of responsibility and unwitting fatherhood plays like a fairy tale—a wondrous reality that feels increasingly distant from what a 30-something can reasonably expect today.

Watching Club Kid, I considered my own parents, who conceived me (by happy accident, I’m told) at 27 and 29. They worked at a pizza restaurant before opening their own bar. My childhood felt wrapped up in Atlanta’s nightlife scene; somehow, completely inexperienced, they still learned to balance their lifestyle with their newfound roles as parents. I lived as the only kid (with my two-years-younger brother) in my parents’ world for several years, often sitting with the bar regulars for hours while my parents worked or being actively watched over by the cooks. It felt like tapping into another world, but it also brought us closer. They didn’t give up their life for me; they made me a part of it. These days, this story sounds like a romantic ideal, a whirlwind adventure only possible without looming loans and the high cost of living. I simply couldn’t imagine doing the same today.

What makes Peter’s story compelling is how he navigates the responsibilities of fatherhood without fully forgetting himself. Some critics have challenged the film, claiming that it’s stigmatizing the queer party scene, but in his telling, he doesn’t rebuke his former lifestyle but rather the parts of it that were bad for him. His friend even welcomes the news of his fatherhood with excitement, spending time with Arlo without passing judgment. Even if he eventually needs to distance himself from these friends’ party habits, it doesn’t need a blowout fight. Life happens, and people move on.

That’s why it’s important that Firstman returns to the real problem at the end of the movie. A vindictive call to social services by Peter’s ex-best friend (Cara Delevigne) leads to Arlo’s deportation and the new father-son duo’s separation. It brings to light the real-life anxieties looming over this heartwarming story. Sure, Peter did not file the proper adoption papers, and his lack of money becomes damningly apparent when he stares down health care premiums and legal bills in the face of an unrelenting bureaucracy.

Firstman refuses to provide a pretty picture. He instead studies the anxieties of young millennials, a generation perennially unsure of the world around them, and distills them into a heartwarming, deferred coming-of-age story. Firstman dreams of who we can become, if only given the chance to grow up in a world where steady ground is hard to find. Peter is growing up alongside his son, hiccups and all.

#6918. A Straight Razor Will Give You the Best Shave of Your Life. Here Are 6 Great Options to Buy.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

If you get razor bumps and ingrowns, don’t be afraid to go old-school.

We’re 120 years past the invention of the safety razor and 50 years past the invention of the cartridge razor. So yes, we understand how annoying it is to even suggest that you should shave with the thing that’s even older—technically as old as shaving itself. Go ahead, make fun of the old-school dandy men’s magazine saying you should be straight-razor shaving with a shaving cream that requires a boar hair brush and smells of tobacco. But to turn shaving from a chore to something you look forward to, well, the straight razor is a one-way ticket to hobby town.

Using an electric razor or beard trimmer is great for getting rid of scruff, but if you ever go for a clean shave, a straight razor is the best tool—full stop. They are the best for your face, don’t give you as many ingrown hairs, and (unlike the five-bladed cartridge razors) don’t get clogged up with hairs after two shaves. Plus they add a sense of elegance to your everyday routine, not to mention a bit of danger, à la Sweeney Todd. Only joking, of course.

So sure, challenge our claim that straight-razor shaving is as good as it gets. Or better yet, try it for a little bit and then tell us we’re wrong. If you clean shave and care about grooming, consider picking up a straight razor and doing it the old-fashioned way.

Frankly, most of these straight razors aren’t even technically straight razors. They are shavettes; hybrids with disposable blades so you don’t have to worry about honing and sharpening (and all the hazards that come with failing to do so). You still get the straight razor vibe, but it’s safer and more convenient. For a starter shavette, you can pick up the Parker SRX straight edge. It’s compatible universally with any double edge blades, so you won’t ever run out.

Take care of this stainless steel beauty and it will retain that sheen for years without a sign of rust. This all makes it a great, affordable pick for intermediate shavers who prioritize performance. For a more gateway razor, I’d pick the Dovo shavette

Fundamentally, this is the same thing as the previous Parker razor. It’s a shavette that uses double-edge disposable blades, so the experience of using it and the accompanying learning curve are the exact same. The difference is the wood handle. It looks great, but if you get anything other than shaving cream on there, it’s certainly not going to be as easy to clean as stainless steel. I still wouldn’t recommend a novice pick this up, but that’s what the safety razor is for.

If you are genuinely just curious about this whole thing, you don’t need to drop even $30 on something you may use once and then put down forever. You can pick up a Classic Samurai razor from Amazon for less than 15 bucks. It’s not the best ever, and you won’t be showing off to your friends, but it’s a way to get your feet wet. Plus it comes with enough extra (safety-razor-compatible) blades that if you do find the knack for straight-razor shaving, you can upgrade to a Parker or Dovo and have plenty of blades to use.

The straight handle is your less flexible, slightly sturdier alternative to your folding shavette. This Feather razor is Japanese made with a straight grip meant to give more fine tuning and control to the average shaver. I only wish that, like Parker shavettes, it was compatible with all double-blade replacements—not just the Feather branded ones.

Whether straight handles are actually easier to control and safer than folding ones is up for debate. Barbers have their preferences and if you are looking to start shaving with a straight razor at home, so will you. If you’ve tried the folding shavettes and can’t quite get a handle (sorry for the pun) on them, this one is worth a shot.

As we exit the shavette category, I turn once again to Dovo. If you want a razor that looks and feels as traditional as it gets while still being brand new, you need a Dovo.

This isn’t for beginners or really even intermediate shavers. If anything, it’s more of a novelty. If you want to use it for real, you should probably be a real barber. If you have the skills (and the disposable income), this will be something fun to whip out for your favorite clients in need of the cleanest shave of their life.

There’s something uniquely satisfying about buying vintage. These come from the era when these were the only razors men used to shave. In a way, that’s how they know they are quality. Just old-fashioned stainless steel blades and a design that modern “retro” can’t come close to replicating.

You can buy these (one per package despite the website’s imagery) sealed or opened and cleaned Friedrich Schlemper straight razors for less than the price of a Dovo. These Sistrum razors are German-made carbon steel, likely dating back to sometime between the 1950s and the 1970s.

Classic Straight Razor: Traditionally, a straight razor has one steel (typically stainless or carbon) blade. In the old days, before the safety and cartridge razors we know and love, this was the only option. Barbers had to hone and sharpen their blades using a leather strap—and if you go classic this is still a requirement. This makes a straight razor an investment that can go south if you don’t take care of it properly. Serious professionals only—it’s not easy to learn how to shave safely with one of these. There’s a reason that as a society, we don’t use them anymore.

Shavettes: This modern hybrid of the safety razor and straight razor is what I recommend over the classic style. They are much more affordable, for starters, and the replaceable blades are much safer, especially for at-home experimentation. If you are a casual looking into straight razors, I cannot stress enough to start and end with a shavette.

Folding vs Straight Handle: In terms of shavettes, it all comes down to preference. A straight handle can be preferable to a lighter, airy folding blade handle due to better ergonomics and greater control. But others find a folding handle to allow them better control and stability. It’s all about whichever you feel allows you more precision while leading to fewer errors.

Esquire has been helping men look (and be) at their best since the heyday of the straight razor. We lived through it. If 90 years of institutional knowledge isn’t going to convince you, nothing will.

#6917. Nicole Kidman Brings Her Youngest Daughter, Faith Margaret, to the Starry Chanel Cruise Show

The pair looked so in sync while showing off their individual styles

When it comes to fashion events, Nicole Kidman tends to brings her eldest biological daughter, Sunday Rose, 18, as her plus-one. This past May, the two walked the red carpet together at the Met Gala in fabulous designer gowns, mere months after Sunday hit the runway for the Dior Spring/Summer 2026 haute couture show. Not to mention, Sunday has also accompanied her mother to events ranging from private Miu Miu parties to the 2024 Paris Summer Olympics.

So, it came as a bit of a surprise when, at the Chanel Cruise 2026-27 show in Sydney, Australia, this morning, Kidman decided instead to bring along her youngest daughter, Faith Margaret Kidman Urban, 15.

At the show, Kidman went classic in a black suit look that was like a deconstructed tuxedo. The ensemble consisted of a cropped black jacket with coattails around back, which she paired with matching straight-leg slacks and layered over contrasting white bases.

Kidman left her jacket open to reveal a thin white vest, which itself was layered over a white tank top. It was a simple, but elegant fit, and the Oscar winner wore a pair of inky satin pumps to round things out.

Faith, meanwhile, looked a bit cozier in a ribbed zip-up jacket in cream, paired with a matching mini skirt. The jacket featured two tiers of gold-studded pockets in front, and it was closed all the way up to its pointy collar. As for accessories, the 15-year-old carried a suede, camel-colored handbag, and she slipped into a pair of strappy silver mules down low.

While in years past Kidman may have been opting to shield her youngest child from the spotlight, Faith has been accompanying her mother to more and more events as of late.

Back in September, the two were seen attending the US Open together, and just last month, Faith sat front row alongside her mom at the Chanel Spring/Summer 2027 ready-to-wear show.

Don’t be surprised if you see more of this high-profile teen at future events.

#6916. These 7 Storage Solutions for Small Spaces Look Too Good to Hide

Display-worthy baskets, glass-fronted cabinets, and colorful tins can make organization part of your decor.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From a Brooklyn studio to a narrow Baltimore rowhouse, two decades of small-space living have taught me how to make every square foot count. Add in my Type A Virgo tendencies and an appreciation for good design, and I have strong opinions about storage: Namely, it should be as good-looking as it is hard-working. My first stop when hunting for products that deliver just that is always IKEA. The Swedish retailer has an unmatched selection of storage furniture and decor. Here are seven pieces that offer both function and form.

I have never known the pleasure of a linen closet, but I’ve gotten so many compliments on this metal-and-glass cabinet in my bathroom that I no longer mind. The reeded glass offers an unexpected texture while obscuring what’s inside to reduce visual clutter, and each interior shelf is roomy enough to hold a stack of towels, plus a small bin for toiletries and medicine bottles. I like to use the bamboo UPPDATERA boxes for material contrast; the larger style works great on the lower shelves to tame my mess of serums, makeup, lotions, and the like.

There is no such thing as too many bins or baskets when you live in a small space. They are the unsung organizational heroes, giving you a way to stash all the miscellaneous things that would typically be tucked away in a closet, attic, basement, or even a spare room in a larger home. IKEA sells a huge variety, but the woven styles like this one are particularly lovely to keep on display. I use them on my EKET living room shelves for games, in my bedroom for scarves and bags, and in the kitchen for tea towels and napkins.

When square footage is at a premium, closets tend to get eliminated. A free-standing wardrobe is the solution to creating clothes storage in a small bedroom. This chic new design features sliding doors—a space-saver itself since you won’t need to account for door clearance—adjustable shelves, and two drawers.

If your front door, like mine, opens directly into your living space, you need to fake a foyer as best you can. The key to that is shoe storage—otherwise, guests will be staring at muddy boots the minute they walk into your home. This cabinet hides 14 pairs behind a traditional design, and sticks out from the wall less than a foot. Two drawers make space for keys and sunglasses while four hooks give bags and dog leashes a home.

All kitchens can benefit from more storage, but it’s the small cook spaces where you really need to get creative. A mobile cart offers that utility, and this one features three shelves for anything from spices and baking supplies to serveware or a coffee bar.

Another simple small kitchen storage idea: Keep your dry goods in stackable containers, like the BETTHAJ tins, to maximize your pantry shelf space. They’re far easier to handle than flimsy paper containers or plastic bags, plus, they look great on a countertop or open shelving. They come in five different sizes, from 19 oz. to 3.3 qt., so you can mix and match to suit your needs.

Why choose between extra seating or more storage when you can have both? This sturdy little pouf hides a compartment that I use for an extra throw blanket, but it could also fit a few games, your knitting supplies, or remote controls. The cushioned top is taut enough that it can also function as a side table, making this a small space triple threat.

Head to IKEA to find storage that maximizes space without sacrificing style.

#6912. Iowa Governor-Elect Rob Sand Was Also a Prada Model

On November 3, as part of the blue-vote tsunami, Iowa elected Democratic Governor Rob Sand. The 44 year-old graduated with a political science degree from Brown University and a J.D. degree from University of Iowa College of Law; he even got a Truman scholarship and an honors thesis. And his 2026 campaign was centered on anti-corruption, bipartisanship, affordable living, and public education. But there’s more—he’s also a pretty face.

As it turns out, the governor-elect helped pay for his prestigious college degrees by walking runways in Milan and Paris. At six-feet, three inches, the young, aspiring politician modeled in shows for Giuliano Fujiwara and Number (N)ine. He even made it into a Prada runway, Newsweek reports. It was the early 2000s, and he was reportedly signed to none other than IMG Models—though his profile seems to have been taken down since.

For whatever reason, his modeling past is not something the chiseled governor-elect likes to talk about, the New York Times noted, but it sure makes for quite the resume.

On January 19, 2004, Sand even missed the Iowa caucus in Decorah because he was modeling menswear in Milan. “It had to be one or the other,” he told Newsweek. “And I chose to go to Europe.” (Who wouldn’t?) While Sand did not win that time around, look at him now, years later, up at the top.

Sand took Iowa this week while in a sharp navy-blue suit and a sky-blue checkered button-down that matched his eyes. “Thank you, Iowa! I love you, Iowa!” he excitedly said after catwalking up to the podium for his victory speech.

Rob Sand’s team has not responded to Harper’s Bazaar’s request for comment.

#6907. We Found the Ultimate Cold-Weather Cologne

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Fulton & Roark's new scent, inspired by a church in Gary, Indiana, is phenomenal.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

Halloween is in the rearview, Daylight Saving Time is over, and the sun has decided it has better places to be (although I am grateful for that extra hour of sleep we got). You know what all that means: It’s officially time to bring out the poofy coats, the cable-knit sweaters, and the winter scents. We’re talking spicy, smoky, sophisticated, and a little dark. Think of it as the olfactory equivalent of a cashmere scarf or quilted jacket. But before you reach for the same old colognes of winters past, consider making room for something new in your fragrance rotation.

Allow me to suggest my new cold-weather go-to, released just last month by one of my all-time favorite niche fragrance houses, Fulton & Roark. This extrait has a pretty sick name: Cathedral of Ruins—and it smells every bit as atmospheric as you’d hope for with a name like that. It’s moody without being gloomy, warm without being cloying, and oh-so intriguing. Put a spritz of this stuff on and you’re guaranteed to start hearing the words, What are you wearing?

Fulton & Roark does what it calls “American fine fragrance,” meaning it doesn’t follow all the rules of your classic European perfumery. The perfumer looks to new ingredients, formulation methods, and scent combinations for all of its fragrances. Oh, and every fragrance is unisex, truly unisex. My boyfriend loves the scents as much as I do, and we often share them.

And since this is American fine fragrance we’re talking about, it only makes sense that each of the brand’s fragrances are named after and inspired by a real American place with a story behind it. That rule even holds for the brand’s name: Fulton is for Fulton County, Georgia; and Roark is for Roark’s Cove in Sewanee, Tennessee. So where is this Cathedral of Ruins? Gary, Indiana. Birthplace of the Jackson 5, and our clearest example of Rust Belt deindustrialization. The once-booming steel city is now also home to thousands of abandoned and decaying buildings, including the City Methodist Church—once one of the grandest churches in the Midwest.

Fulton and Roark co-founder Kevin Keller showed me photos of what City Methodist Church looks like now, which he took on his own trip to Gary, and the immediate sense I got from these photos was a devastating sort of beauty (or a beautiful sort of devastation). The church’s roof has fallen in; the only living congregation is the vegetation that reclaims more and more of the space every year. But, its Neo-Gothic stone walls still stand. It’s a dead relic, but it is not a dead space. It pulls up mixed feelings. This Cathedral of Ruins extrait de parfum is Fulton & Roark’s homage to it, and it nails everything you get from seeing a picture of the place.

Like I said, Cathedral of Ruins is the perfect cold-weather scent. But instead of the typical rich, gourmand notes you get in fall and winter fragrances like vanilla, caramel, and amber, Cathedral of Ruins relies on spices, smoke, and woods to create a warm atmosphere. This is not a syrupy sweet winter scent—it’s smoldering, Gothic-inspired, sophisticated, and unlike any scent you’ve ever smelled before.

At the top, you get resinous cypress, spicy black peppercorn, and, unexpectedly, floral rose. The lush, velvety notes of rose lead into the more delicate floral notes of saffron, symbolizing the grace of the cathedral (and possibly the new natural growth of vegetation over the ruins). Rich Peru balsam finishes out the heart with more warm, woody vibes.

But the base is where this fragrance really stands out. The star of the show here is burned frankincense, the classic scent of sacred ritual—imagine smoke rising above the walls, past the ceiling that’s no longer there. Frankincense is, obviously, smoky, but not in the same way as something like tobacco; the frankincense here is dry, resinous, and subtly fresh like pine. To me, frankincense even has a waxy-green quality similar to olives. The warm depth of leathered woods and musks finish out the fragrance, leaving the intrigue of smoky woods to linger on your skin.
